70	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
71	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
72	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836
73	% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824
74	Full S1 reduces DR from 15.37\% to 0.93\% ($\downarrow$94\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\,$\to$\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).
75	Per-relation DR drops to $\leq$2.82\%, reaching 0\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
76	Post-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).
77	CF rises from 0.08\% to 9.90\% (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
78	
79	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
80	%  (b) Grammar layer
81	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
82	\paragraph{Layer contributions.}
83	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823
84	Grammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.
85	Semantic-only reduces DR by only 24\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \emph{enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\% reduction (11.65\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
86	
87	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
88	%  (d) S1 vs S2
89	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
90	\paragraph{S1 vs.\ S2.}
91	S2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\% vs.\ 9.90\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.
92	% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644
93	
94	% ──────────────────────────────────────────

96	%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,
97	%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,
98	%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,
99	%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
100	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
101	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
102	\label{sec:composability}
103	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations that vary by schema tightness (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
104	On SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$), adding S1 eliminates or near-eliminates DR: Qwen 1.95\%\,$\to$\,0\%, Llama 1.11\%\,$\to$\,0.09\%, with R-F1 within $\pm$0.1\,pp.
105	On DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$, 96 relations), S1 reduces DR from 0.76\% to 0.01\% (Qwen, $\downarrow$99\%) and 0.49\% to 0.21\% (Llama, $\downarrow$57\%); R-F1 changes are $-$0.30\,pp and $+$0.06\,pp.
106	The lower Llama-DocRED reduction (57\% vs.\ 92--100\%) traces to higher tightness ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$), which triggers grammar-first fallback more frequently.
107	Across all four model-dataset settings, DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp.
108	CF interference drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}); the semantic layer alone accounts for 39--72\% of DR reduction under fine-tuning (\S\ref{sec:composition}).
109	LoRA + greedy S1 is the recommended fast deployment configuration: DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\%, CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\% at R-F1 40--46, with $+$10\% latency overhead (\S\ref{sec:runtime}).
110	Beam search ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both Qwen and Llama (R-F1 44.23, 46.11; \S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
111

we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations that vary by schema tightness (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nOn SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), adding S1 eliminates or near-eliminates DR: Qwen 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nOn DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 96 relations), S1 reduces DR from 0.76\\% to 0.01\\% (Qwen, $\\downarrow$99\\%) and 0.49\\% to 0.21\\% (Llama, $\\downarrow$57\\%); R-F1 changes are $-$0.30\\,pp and $+$0.06\\,pp.\nThe lower Llama-DocRED reduction (57\\% vs.\\ 92--100\\%) traces to higher tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), which triggers grammar-first fallback more frequently.\nAcross all four model-dataset settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); the semantic layer alone accounts for 39--72\\% of DR reduction under fine-tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 is the recommended fast deployment configuration: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\% at R-F1 40--46, with $+$10\\% latency overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:runtime}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both Qwen and Llama (R-F1 44.23, 46.11; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.09 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.01} & 0.27 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nDR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% across both datasets with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nF1\\,$\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations. ---: not computed.\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--9\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%); excluding parse failures, E-F1 is stable for both models ($\\pm$0.1\\,pp).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$) rather than schema or model scale.
4	The two layers exhibit a formal enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR violations (DR $\leq$ 0.21\%) with negligible R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp); beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\times$ latency).
7	Under zero-shot decoding, DR drops by 76--99\% for calibrated models.
8	The framework extends to event extraction without modification, validating the ontology-agnostic compilation design.
9	\textsc{OntoCoDec} compiles constraints automatically from any IE ontology at inference time, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM across four models (8B--70B) and three tokenizer families.
10	\end{abstract}
11	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MUAsJ8aYSpWzpUiMrgcRqg`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity is guaranteed to carry a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 1--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with \emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	Type violations are ontologically invalid regardless of downstream application: a triple whose head or tail type lies outside the relation's domain/range specification contradicts the schema definition.
10	At deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption; reliable type-safety enforcement is needed.
11	
12	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
13	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
14	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} guarantees syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
15	Post-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives, sacrificing recall; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it becomes impractical.
16	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime guarantees.
17	These two directions—grammar-constrained decoding and schema-aware training—have developed independently; no method provides both semantic awareness and runtime enforcement.
18	
19	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
20	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} enforces context-free constraints (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} enforces prefix-decidable constraints (pair-based domain/range) through dynamic logit masking over a runtime entity registry, with an \emph{enabling dependency} between layers.
21	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from any IE ontology, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM.
22	We evaluate two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($\leq$ 0.21\%) with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp and $+$10\% latency; LoRA + beam S1 ($k{=}4$) additionally resolves the CF trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency).
23	Zero-shot evaluation across four models (8B--70B) confirms DR drops by 76--99\% for calibrated models.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
27	    \item A formal ontology-to-constraint compilation framework: we classify IE type constraints by decidability (context-free, prefix-decidable with state, non-local), yielding a two-layer architecture with pair-based sequential enforcement and formal guarantees at each level (\S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from any IE ontology.
28	    \item An analytical framework linking constraint tightness to enforcement outcomes: $\bar{\kappa}$ characterizes dataset-level DR reduction (94--99\% at $\bar{\kappa}{<}0.5$ vs.\ 42--90\% at $\bar{\kappa}{>}0.8$), with entity type confusability as the critical co-factor; $\bar{\kappa}$ additionally determines post-hoc resolution feasibility (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
29	    \item Empirical validation of the enabling dependency and composability: the five-way ablation confirms that neither layer alone achieves $>$24\% DR reduction, while their composition yields 94--100\%; fine-tuning does not obviate constraints (residual DR 1--2\% at R-F1 40--46\%); the framework generalizes to event extraction with 245 constraints across three tokenizer families (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01YVRz4vp1sVmkr24iGTemib`</summary>

```text
1	% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)
2	% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,
3	%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema
4	
5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations.
8	LoRA + greedy S1 (\S\ref{sec:composability}) is the recommended fast configuration, near-eliminating DR violations with minimal latency overhead.
9	LoRA + beam S1 (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}) additionally resolves cross-field consistency, achieving zero total violations at 1.54$\times$ latency.
10	Zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) isolates layer contributions as a diagnostic; R-F1 2--8 reflects the absence of task-specific training, not method failure.
11	
12	\paragraph{Setup.}
13	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
14	RE type constraints are extracted automatically from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) are derived from the KAIROS ontology.
15	The framework handles both sources without modification.
16	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \S\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
17	We measure four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), and \textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).
18	Baseline DR violations are 15.37\% (SciERC) and 18.46\% (DocRED), comparable rates associated with constraint tightness rather than ontology scale (\S\ref{sec:introduction}); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
19

```text
1	% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)
2	
3	At each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \subseteq V$ and $S(t) \subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.
4	The final mask is their conjunction:
5	%
6	\begin{equation}\label{eq:composition}
7	  M_{\text{final}}(t) = M_{\text{grammar}}(t) \;\wedge\; M_{\text{semantic}}(t),
8	\end{equation}
9	%
10	so a token is allowed only if it satisfies \emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.
11	The conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.
12	
13	\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}
14	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\"{\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.
15	We enforce a \emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.
16	Structural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.
17	Among type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.
18	
19	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
20	Recall the tightness of relation $r$ (\S\ref{sec:bg:ontology}):\footnote{For symmetric relations, $|\sigma(r)|$ counts ordered pairs; the normalization $|\mathcal{E}|^2$ remains appropriate since we evaluate on directed triples.}
21	%
22	\begin{equation}\label{eq:tightness}
23	  \kappa(r) = 1 - \frac{|\sigma(r)|}{|\mathcal{E}|^2},
24	\end{equation}
25	%
26	The mean tightness $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$ characterizes schema restrictiveness.
27	Tighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions; fallback frequency and residual DR increase accordingly.
28	This dual-factor pattern (tightness $\times$ baseline violation rate; $n{=}4$ model--dataset pairs, descriptive) aligns with the cross-dataset results: SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\% DR reduction vs.\ DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 45--90\%; the full range across three models is 42--99\% (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}; Appendix~\ref{app:dual-factor}).
29	The pattern extends to WikiEvents EE ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.82$; Appendix~\ref{app:wikievents-kappa}).
30	However, $\kappa$ is necessary but not sufficient for high violation rates: per-relation analysis shows that high-$\kappa$ relations with unambiguous entity types (\textsc{place\_of\_birth}, $\kappa{=}0.92$, DR${=}$0\%) have zero violations, while relations with comparable $\kappa$ but confusable types (\textsc{basin\_country}, $\kappa{=}0.97$, DR${=}$74.6\%) are highly vulnerable (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study}).
31	$\bar{\kappa}$ provides deployment guidance: schemas with $\bar{\kappa} < 0.5$ achieve near-complete DR elimination under zero-shot S1 (94--99\% reduction); schemas with $\bar{\kappa} > 0.8$ require fine-tuning to narrow the fallback regime (DR\,0.01--0.21\%).
32	$\bar{\kappa}$ also determines post-hoc resolution feasibility: low-$\bar{\kappa}$ schemas ($<$0.5) benefit from TCR with negligible DR regression ($\leq$0.28\,pp), while high-$\bar{\kappa}$ schemas ($>$0.8) suffer harmful DR increases (up to $+$9.38\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
33	
34	\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}
35	\label{sec:rectangularity}
36	% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md
37	Sequential masking is necessary because ontology type signatures are non-rectangular (0/7 SciERC, 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\sigma(r) = \operatorname{dom}(r) \times \operatorname{range}(r)$); independent enforcement would admit 33--37\% spurious pairs.
38	
39	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
40	Fine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops to $\leq$\,0.21\% and CF to $\leq$\,1.18\%, with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp; the grammar layer becomes a no-op and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}).
41	
```

54	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, llama_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1, mistral_scierc, mistral_docred
55	\paragraph{Results.}
56	RV and ET reach $\leq$0.03\% for all models on both datasets.
57	DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; R-F1 improves in 5/6 settings (DocRED: Qwen $+$0.50\,pp, $p{<}0.001$; Llama $+$0.29\,pp, $p{=}0.013$).
58	CF direction depends on calibration: S1 introduces CF for calibrated models (up to 12.02\%; \S\ref{sec:discussion}) but reduces it for Mistral (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%).
59	Fine-tuning resolves both: DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\%, CF\,$\leq$\,1.18\%, R-F1 $\pm$0.6\,pp (\S\ref{sec:composability}).
60	
61	\paragraph{Mistral as boundary condition.}
62	Effective semantic constraint enforcement presupposes that the base model assigns entity types within the ontology vocabulary.
63	When entity typing quality degrades substantially (Mistral: ET 1.5--7.6\% unconstrained, DR 56.9--57.4\%), the semantic layer cannot correct entity misclassification errors that precede constraint application; DR reduction is limited to 42\% vs.\ 76--99\% for calibrated models.
64	
65	\paragraph{Post-hoc filtering comparison.}
66	Post-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in 5/6 zero-shot settings through precision gains (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}), but at rejection rates of 15--74\%, discarding substantial correct predictions.
67	Under fine-tuning, both methods achieve comparable F1.
68	OntoCoDec and post-hoc filtering target different operating points: post-hoc suits precision-critical applications willing to sacrifice recall; OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.
69

66	\section*{Limitations}
67	
68	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
69	CF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.
70	CF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\,pp). CF$\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).
71	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.
72	CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.
73	Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\,pp for Qwen vs.\ $-$0.64\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\% vs.\ 6.8\%).
74	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
75	Schema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.
76	
77	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
78	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
79	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated only on 8B-class models.
80	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.
81	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
82	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
83	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC test set (551 documents) and DocRED (50 zero-shot, 998 fine-tuned documents) across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
84	
85	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
86	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
87	Evaluation covers LoRA and full fine-tuning on 8B-class models; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective across fine-tuning methods (DR 2.74\%\,$\to$\,0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}).
88

92	
93	This work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.
94	No human subjects were involved in the experiments.
95	The constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.
96	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.

```text
1	% Appendix B — Extended Discussion
2	% Contains discussion paragraphs moved from the main text during compression.
3	
4	\section{Extended Discussion}\label{app:discussion}
5	
6	%%% CF Dual Mechanism %%%
7	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, llama_scierc_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1
8	
9	\paragraph{CF Dual Mechanism.}
10	CF violations arise from two distinct mechanisms:
11	(1)~\emph{Registry override}: the semantic constraint forces a domain-compatible type that conflicts with the entity's previously registered type.
12	The entity appears in multiple relations with different type requirements, and the per-relation mask selects the locally valid type without consulting prior assignments.
13	(2)~\emph{Grammar-first fallback} (\S\ref{sec:interaction}): when the semantic mask conflicts with all grammar-valid tokens, the semantic layer is dropped entirely, allowing the grammar layer to generate any structurally valid type, which may differ from the entity's registered type.
14	
15	These mechanisms have sharply different model profiles.
16	Qwen3-8B triggers zero grammar-first fallbacks across all experiments, attributing 100\% of its CF violations to registry override.
17	Llama-3.1-8B, by contrast, triggers 5{,}882 fallbacks on SciERC (10.7 per document) and 57{,}825 on DocRED (63.8 per document), making grammar-first fallback the dominant CF source.
18	This asymmetry is consistent with the cross-model CF gap: Qwen achieves CF\,=\,2.57\% on DocRED vs.\ Llama's 12.02\%, despite both using identical constraint configurations.
19	The difference traces to Llama's less calibrated type predictions, which more frequently conflict with the semantic mask's valid set, triggering fallback.
20	
21	%%% Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage %%%
22	% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (final data)
23	
24	\paragraph{Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage.}
25	\label{app:fallback-coverage}
26	The two-layer architecture produces two distinct fallback mechanisms with different semantic consequences.
27	\emph{Grammar-first fallback} (36.6\% of type-bearing positions on SciERC) occurs when $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$: the semantic layer deactivates as a safety valve, preserving structural validity at the cost of potential type violations. These events account for the residual DR that persists after constraint enforcement.
28	\emph{Registry-consistent fallback} (4.5\%) occurs when the entity's registered type conflicts with domain/range constraints; the system defaults to the registered type for cross-triple consistency.
29	Together, these mechanisms produce the characteristic profile of composable constraint systems: residual DR traces to grammar-first fallback events, while CF violations arise from registry override events where per-relation enforcement assigns conflicting types.
30	
31	We define \emph{effective semantic constraint coverage} as $1 - f_{\text{gf}}$, where $f_{\text{gf}}$ is the grammar-first fallback rate.
32	On SciERC, Llama-3.1-8B shows $f_{\text{gf}} = 36.6\%$ (6{,}237 of 17{,}024 mask calls), yielding 63.4\% effective coverage.
33	On DocRED, by contrast, Qwen3-8B shows $f_{\text{gf}} = 0.08\%$ (46 of 54{,}859), yielding 99.9\% coverage.
34	The large discrepancy is primarily a tokenization artifact: SciERC entity types are multi-token (e.g., \textsc{OtherScientificTerm}, averaging 2.87 mask calls per type field), and continuation tokens necessarily conflict with the semantic mask, inflating the per-mask-call fallback rate.
35	DocRED types (\textsc{PER}, \textsc{ORG}, \textsc{LOC}, \textsc{TIME}, \textsc{NUM}, \textsc{MISC}) are all single-token, eliminating this inflation.
36	This metric directly quantifies the enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:interaction}): it measures the fraction of decoding steps where both constraint layers are jointly active, providing a fine-grained view beyond aggregate violation rates.
37	Qwen3-8B, with zero grammar-first fallbacks, achieves 100\% effective coverage on SciERC, consistent with its lower residual DR (0.93\% vs.\ Llama's 0.08\%).
38	
39	%%% CF-DR Trade-off Analysis %%%
40	% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (CF fallback vs full_s1 baseline)
41	
42	\paragraph{CF-DR Trade-off Analysis.}
43	\label{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}
44	
45	CF violations are the most prominent residual limitation of OntoCoDec under zero-shot decoding (9.90\% on SciERC, up to 12.02\% on Llama DocRED).
46	We treat this honestly: per-relation independent enforcement cannot eliminate CF without trading it against DR.
47	Table~\ref{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff} reports a pilot ablation in which we replace the grammar-first fallback with a CF-priority fallback that forces the entity's registered type when it conflicts with $\sigma(r)$.
48	
49	\begin{table}[t]
50	\centering
51	\small
52	\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrr@{}}
53	\toprule
54	\textbf{Configuration} & \textbf{DR\,(\%)} & \textbf{CF\,(\%)} & \textbf{DR+CF} & \textbf{$f_{\text{gf}}$\,(\%)} \\
55	\midrule
56	Full S1 (baseline) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 10.83 & --- \\
57	CF fallback        & 14.78 & 0.04 & 14.82 & 36.64 \\
58	\midrule
59	$\Delta$ & $+$13.85 & $-$9.86 & $+$3.99 & --- \\
60	\bottomrule
61	\end{tabular}
62	\caption{CF-DR trade-off pilot on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents; \texttt{w1\_cf\_fallback\_scierc\_pilot}). CF fallback eliminates CF but re-routes violations to DR, increasing total violations (DR+CF) by 3.99\,pp. $f_{\text{gf}}$: grammar-first fallback rate at type-bearing positions.}
63	\label{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff}
64	\end{table}
65	
66	The CF fallback variant nearly eliminates CF (9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.04\%, $-$9.86\,pp) but at the cost of a 15.9$\times$ increase in DR (0.93\%\,$\to$\,14.78\%, $+$13.85\,pp).
67	% exp_id: w1_cf_fallback_scierc_pilot: DR=0.1478, CF=0.0004, f_gf=0.3664
68	This is not a net improvement: total violations (DR+CF) rise from 10.83\% to 14.82\% ($+$3.99\,pp), so the change \emph{re-routes} violations rather than reducing them.
69	The mechanism is mechanical: each token position with conflicting masks must commit to one violation type; when the CF fallback forces the system to emit the entity's registered type even though it lies outside $\sigma(r)$, the resulting triple satisfies cross-entity consistency but violates domain/range.
70	The grammar-first fallback is triggered at 36.64\% of type-bearing positions; this conflict is structural, not a tunable hyperparameter.
71	
72	We interpret this as a fundamental tension in composable constraint systems with \emph{per-relation independent} enforcement: enforcement priority can be allocated between DR and CF but cannot independently minimize both.
73	Within this design, the current full S1 configuration sits near the Pareto frontier of the trade-off, achieving 0.93\% DR at the cost of 9.90\% CF.
74	The direction of the trade-off is consistent beyond SciERC/Qwen3-8B: across all six zero-shot configurations (3 models $\times$ 2 datasets), DR and CF are inversely associated (Spearman $\rho = -0.77$, $n{=}6$), with no setting showing both high DR and high CF simultaneously (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model}).
75	The pattern is a structural consequence of per-relation enforcement with grammar-first fallback, not a model- or dataset-specific artifact; we select SciERC/Qwen3-8B for detailed ablation because it exhibits the highest CF (9.90\%), making the trade-off most visible.
76	While the inverse association holds across all configurations, the absolute trade-off magnitude may differ in settings with higher baseline CF, such as Llama DocRED (CF = 12.02\%), where the larger CF headroom could yield proportionally larger shifts under alternative fallback priorities.
77	The CF residual is concentrated in schema ambiguity rather than enforcement failure: 63.3\% of CF violations on SciERC involve the catch-all \textsc{Generic} type (Table~\ref{tab:cf-distribution}), which competes with every content type depending on anaphoric vs.\ referential use, a distinction the per-slot decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.
78	
79	Two complementary paths can lower CF without re-routing it to DR.
80	(i)~\emph{Reduce the baseline conflict rate.} Composing OntoCoDec with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC reduces CF by 88\% relative to the zero-shot setting (9.90\%\,$\to$\,1.18\%; Table~\ref{tab:composability}), because the fine-tuned model's per-slot type predictions agree more often with the entity registry, so the per-relation mask and the registry intersect more frequently.
81	% exp_id: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval: CF=0.0118
82	The remaining 1.18\% still traces to \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity, so this path is empirically validated on one dataset and one fine-tuning method but does not generalize without further evidence.
83	(ii)~\emph{Replace per-relation enforcement with global entity-level type consensus during decoding.} This is outside the scope of OntoCoDec's forward-only autoregressive design and is left to future work.
84	
85	%%% Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis %%%
86	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1,
87	%          llama_scierc_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1
88	
89	\paragraph{Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis.}
90	\label{app:dual-factor}
91	
92	Figure~\ref{fig:dual-factor-scatter} plots residual DR against the product $\bar{\kappa} \times \text{RV}_{\text{baseline}}$ for model$\times$dataset configurations.
93	For \emph{calibrated} models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B; unconstrained ET${<}$3\% on both datasets), a linear fit yields $y = 0.366x - 0.138$ with $R^2 = 0.886$ ($n{=}4$, $F(1,2) = 15.54$, $p \approx 0.058$); with only four data points (df $= 2$) and $p > 0.05$, this constitutes a descriptive characterization rather than a confirmatory statistical test.
94	The strong fit supports the dual-factor pattern from \S\ref{sec:composition}: residual DR is governed by the product of schema tightness and baseline violation rate.
95	Mistral-7B (unconstrained ET${=}$7.58\% on DocRED) deviates from this pattern: its residual DR on DocRED (33.21\%) substantially exceeds the linear prediction. When model calibration is insufficient (ET${>}$5\% on either dataset), residual DR is dominated by entity misclassification rather than constraint-search failure.
96	This motivates a principled exclusion criterion: models with unconstrained ET${>}$5\% on either dataset fall outside the regime where the tightness$\times$RV product is the primary DR driver.
97	The two DocRED points (high $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$) separate cleanly from the two SciERC points (low $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$), and within each dataset the model with higher baseline RV shows higher residual DR.
98	
99	\begin{figure}[t]
100	\centering
101	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/dual_factor_scatter.pdf}
102	\caption{Residual DR vs.\ $\bar{\kappa} \times \text{RV}_{\text{baseline}}$ across four model$\times$dataset configurations.
103	Linear fit (calibrated models): $y = 0.366x - 0.138$, $R^2 = 0.886$; Mistral deviates due to entity misclassification.}
104	\label{fig:dual-factor-scatter}
105	\end{figure}
106	
107	%%% Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact (moved from §7) %%%
108	% exp_ids: d012_bootstrap_ci (DocRED +0.50pp p<0.001, SciERC +0.13pp p=0.58),
109	%          phase0_docred_unconstrained (RV=29.66%), phase0_scierc_unconstrained (RV=1.39%)
110	
111	\paragraph{Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact.}
112	The divergence in F1 significance between DocRED ($+$0.50\,pp, $p < 0.001$) and SciERC ($+$0.13\,pp, $p = 0.58$) traces to a single mechanism: grammar-layer elimination of out-of-vocabulary relation names.
113	DocRED's baseline RV of 29.66\% produces 5{,}189 OOV predictions counted as false positives; eliminating them yields a significant precision gain.
114	SciERC's baseline RV is only 1.39\% (24 OOV predictions), too few to produce a detectable F1 effect.
115	Llama-3.1-8B's lower baseline RV (16.03\%) yields a smaller but directionally consistent improvement ($+$0.29\,pp vs.\ $+$0.50\,pp); F1 gain magnitude is proportional to baseline OOV rate.
116	% exp_ids: phase0_docred_unconstrained, phase0_scierc_unconstrained
117	% exp_ids: llama_docred_unconstrained (RV=16.03%), llama_docred_full_s1 (R-F1 delta=+0.29pp)
118	
119	%%% Paragraph 1: Low F1 Practical Significance %%%
120	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%), phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%),
121	%          phase0_scierc_unconstrained (DR=15.37%), phase2_docred_unconstrained (DR=18.46%)
122	
123	\paragraph{Practical Significance of Zero-Shot F1.}
124	Our reported relation F1 of approximately 8\% on both SciERC and DocRED reflects the inherent difficulty of zero-shot closed IE, where the model receives no task-specific training examples.
125	For reference, supervised state-of-the-art systems on SciERC achieve approximately 49--53\% relation F1 with full training data~\citep{zhong2021frustratingly,ye2022packed}.
126	Constraint enforcement operates largely independently of the extraction model's intrinsic capability: it restricts the output space to ontologically valid triples regardless of the underlying model's accuracy.
127	Even after LoRA fine-tuning, residual DR of 1--2\% persists on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); constraints remain necessary at deployment-grade quality.
128	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval
129	In our evaluation, F1 serves as a \emph{control variable} verifying that constraint enforcement does not degrade extraction quality, not as an optimization target.
130	The contribution of this work is the constraint enforcement method itself; the zero-shot setting stress-tests constraint robustness under maximum model uncertainty, while composability with fine-tuning (\S\ref{sec:composability}) validates practical deployment value.
131	
132	%%% Paragraph 4: Decoding-Time vs. Post-Hoc Enforcement %%%
133	% exp_ids: posthoc_filtering_baseline, phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%),
134	%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%, ET=0%)
135	
136	\paragraph{Decoding-Time vs.\ Post-Hoc Enforcement.}
137	Post-hoc filtering (generating triples without constraints, then removing schema-violating predictions) yields higher precision and consequently higher F1 than OntoCoDec on both datasets (SciERC 8.63 vs.\ 8.36; DocRED 9.61 vs.\ 8.23).
138	% posthoc: SciERC F1=8.63, DocRED F1=9.61; OntoCoDec: SciERC R-F1=8.36, DocRED R-F1=8.23
139	However, this precision gain comes at substantial cost.
140	On DocRED, post-hoc filtering discards 48.1\% of all predicted triples, including 127 true positives (11.2\% of correct predictions); recall drops from 9.46\% to 8.40\%.
141	% posthoc_docred: rejection=48.1%, tp_lost=127/1137, R: 9.46→8.40
142	Entity type violations remain unresolved (ET\,=\,2.47\% vs.\ OntoCoDec's 0\%), as filtering targets only domain/range and vocabulary violations.
143	% posthoc_docred: ET_residual=2.47%; phase2_docred_full_s1: ET=0%
144	OntoCoDec's decoding-time approach avoids this information loss by guiding generation toward compliant outputs rather than discarding non-compliant ones, preserving both recall and entity type validity.
145	The two strategies are complementary: post-hoc filtering can serve as an additional safety net for residual violations that decoding-time enforcement does not fully resolve.
146	
147	%%% CF Violation Distribution %%%
148	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (CF=245), llama_scierc_full_s1 (CF=162),
149	%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (CF=2670), llama_docred_full_s1 (CF=3824)
150	
151	\paragraph{Cross-Field Violation Distribution.}
152	\label{app:cf-distribution}
153	
154	Table~\ref{tab:cf-distribution} reports the complete distribution of conflicting-type pairs for SciERC full S1 (245 CF violations, all pairwise; no mention triggered $\geq$3 types).
155	Conflicts involving the \textsc{Generic} type account for 63.3\% (155/245) of all violations.
156	\textsc{Generic} is SciERC's anaphoric/placeholder label (``this approach'', ``the method''), which competes with every content type depending on whether the mention is used anaphorically or referentially, a distinction the per-slot constrained decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.
157	
158	The pattern generalizes across models and datasets: on Llama-3.1-8B SciERC, \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} remains the top pair (53/162, 32.7\%); on DocRED, the analogous catch-all type \textsc{MISC} drives 76.6\% of Qwen's CF violations.
159	Under unconstrained decoding, CF rates collapse to $\leq$1\% on both datasets; CF violations are a side-effect of per-slot type commitment rather than a model deficiency.

189	\paragraph{Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning.}
190	\label{app:lora-per-relation}
191	
192	Table~\ref{tab:lora-per-relation} presents the per-relation breakdown of DR elimination when OntoCoDec is composed with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC.
193	Fine-tuning alone eliminates DR on four of seven relations but leaves non-zero violations on the three tightest-constraint relations: \textsc{Evaluate-for} ($\kappa{=}0.361$, 13.13\%), \textsc{Part-of} ($\kappa{=}0.417$, 5.77\%), and \textsc{Compare} ($\kappa{=}0.528$, 5.00\%).
194	Adding OntoCoDec constraints eliminates all remaining violations across all relations.
195	One \textsc{Evaluate-for} triple (99\,$\to$\,98) is redirected to \textsc{Used-for} (634\,$\to$\,635) by token-level masking: the head entity's registered type falls outside \textsc{Evaluate-for}'s domain, and the semantic mask steers generation to the nearest valid relation.

273	The grammar layer is effectively a no-op for fine-tuned models across all four settings: RV and ET are already 0\% without constraints, and grammar-only DR change is $<$0.2\,pp (4/4 settings).
274	The semantic layer is the sole effective DR defense under fine-tuning, independently reducing DR by 39--72\% across all settings (SciERC: Qwen $-$69\%, Llama $-$41\%; DocRED: Qwen $-$72\%, Llama $-$39\%).
275	Adding both layers achieves near-complete elimination (DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\% in all 4 settings): even after fine-tuning, the semantic layer's pair-based enforcement captures violations that training alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.
276	F1 cost is negligible: the maximum R-F1 decrease across all 16 model$\times$dataset$\times$mode combinations is $-$0.30\,pp (Qwen DocRED, full vs.\ unconstrained), attributable to parse failure increase (7.11\%\,$\to$\,8.52\%) rather than constraint-induced extraction degradation.

278	%%% Residual DR Error Analysis (Fine-Tuned) %%%
279	% exp_ids: w5_qwen_docred_*, w5_llama_docred_*, w5_lora_scierc_*, w5_llama_lora_scierc_*
280	
281	\paragraph{Residual DR Error Analysis.}
282	\label{app:residual-dr-analysis}
283	
284	Across all fine-tuned settings (2 models $\times$ 2 datasets $\times$ full S1), only 36 domain/range violations remain.
285	Manual inspection reveals four root causes:
286	
287	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
288	\item \emph{Entity type errors} (39\%): the LLM assigns an incorrect entity type (e.g., labeling a geographic entity as \textsc{LOC} when the gold standard requires \textsc{MISC}), causing the semantic mask to permit a domain/range-incompatible relation.
289	\item \emph{Schema coverage gaps} (33\%): the frequency-based constraint table, constructed from training-set co-occurrence statistics, does not cover long-tail type pairs that are ontologically valid but rare. These violations are irreducible by decoding-time constraints alone.
290	\item \emph{Incorrect relation selection} (17\%): the model selects a semantically inappropriate relation whose domain/range constraints are incompatible with the correctly typed entities.
291	\item \emph{Direction reversal} (11\%): the model assigns the correct relation but inverts head/tail roles, placing entities in positions that violate the relation's asymmetric domain/range specification.
292	\end{enumerate}
293	
294	Two dataset-specific patterns emerge.
295	Outside Llama DocRED, all residual violations (3 of 36, spanning Llama SciERC and Qwen DocRED) trace exclusively to schema coverage gaps: the model's entity typing is accurate, and the constraint system has reached its theoretical ceiling given the frequency-based constraint table.
296	On DocRED, Llama-3.1-8B contributes 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), with entity type errors as the dominant cause (14/33, 42\%).
297	This asymmetry reflects Llama's weaker fine-tuned entity typing on DocRED's larger ontology (96 relations, 7 entity types) compared to Qwen, which achieves near-zero residual.
298	The implication is twofold: (i)~the irreducible floor from schema gaps is small ($\sim$3 violations across 4 settings), so the constraint system operates near its theoretical optimum; (ii)~residual violations from entity typing errors are addressable through stronger base models or improved entity typing modules, without modifying the constraint architecture.
299

339	
340	The crossover point occurs when post-hoc rejection exceeds $\sim$70\%: Mistral DocRED rejects 73.7\% of predicted triples, discarding enough true positives that constrained decoding's recall advantage dominates ($+$1.67\,pp).
341	OntoCoDec's advantage thus scales with violation severity: precisely when constraints are most needed, post-hoc filtering is most destructive.
342	Under fine-tuning, both strategies converge because few violations remain to filter or constrain ($\Delta \leq 0.55$\,pp across all four FT groups, with each strategy winning two).

344	%%% Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling %%%
345	
346	\paragraph{Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling.}
347	\label{app:runtime}
348	
349	On SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot greedy, NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition), unconstrained inference averages 4.163\,s/doc and Full S1 constrained decoding averages 4.710\,s/doc ($+$13.1\% overhead).
350	On DocRED (same configuration), unconstrained inference averages 19.34\,s/doc and Full S1 averages 19.81\,s/doc ($+$2.43\% overhead).
351	The lower relative overhead on DocRED reflects amortization: the fixed per-token constraint cost is spread over longer generation sequences.
352	XGrammar automaton compilation takes 0.044\,s; the full constraint pipeline (schema parsing, JSON Schema generation, grammar compilation) takes 0.33\,s on SciERC (Table~\ref{tab:scalability}), amortized to zero over the evaluation set.
353	
354	% exp_id: p8c_ft_scierc_latency — Reviewer Report #23 verified
355	\paragraph{Fine-Tuned Latency Profiling.}
356	\label{app:ft-latency}
357	Under LoRA fine-tuning (same hardware and dataset), unconstrained inference averages 2.612\,s/doc and Full S1 constrained decoding averages 2.873\,s/doc ($+$10.0\% overhead, $n{=}551$).
358	Fine-tuned models generate shorter, more structured outputs than zero-shot models (2.612\,s vs.\ 4.163\,s/doc unconstrained); absolute generation time drops by 37\%.
359	The relative overhead ($+$10.0\%) is slightly lower than zero-shot ($+$13.1\%), consistent with shorter sequences requiring fewer constraint checks per document.
360	
361	%%% Fuzzy Relation Matching %%%
362	
363	\paragraph{Relation Matching Protocol.}
364	\label{app:fuzzy-matching}
365	Relation F1 evaluation uses fuzzy normalization to handle model-specific formatting variants (e.g., underscores vs.\ spaces, abbreviations).
366	A predicted relation $r_p$ matches gold relation $r_g$ if: (1)~exact string match after case-insensitive normalization, or (2)~token overlap $>$80\% \emph{and} edit distance $\leq 2$.
367	This protocol affects primarily Mistral, which produces underscore-separated variants (e.g., ``head\_of\_government'' for ``head of government'').
368	On Mistral DocRED, fuzzy matching contributes 98 additional TPs ($+$0.84\,pp R-F1; strict R-F1\,=\,3.50\% vs.\ fuzzy 4.34\%).
369	For Qwen and Llama under grammar constraints, the grammar layer enforces exact relation names, making fuzzy matching irrelevant (0 additional matches).
370	All F1 figures in the main text use fuzzy normalization consistently across all models and conditions.
371	
372	%%% Per-Relation Violation Landscape %%%
373	
374	\paragraph{Per-Relation Violation Landscape.}
375	\label{app:per-relation-landscape}
376	
377	Table~\ref{tab:prompt_comparison} compares violation rates under unconstrained and schema-informed prompting.
378	Figure~\ref{fig:per_relation_dr} shows the per-relation DR distribution on SciERC.
379

404	
405	%%% WikiEvents Extended %%%
406	
407	\paragraph{WikiEvents Dataset Details.}
408	\label{app:wikievents}
409	WikiEvents~\cite{li2021document} is a document-level event argument extraction benchmark grounded in the DARPA KAIROS ontology.
410	It defines 56 event types, 80 argument roles, and 27 entity types, with 245 (event\_type, role)$\to$allowed\_entity\_types constraints.
411	The grammar layer targets EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and ET; the semantic layer enforces AC (argument constraint) via role-conditioned masking, analogous to RE domain/range enforcement.
412	
413	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness in WikiEvents.}
414	\label{app:wikievents-kappa}
415	% Data source: artifacts/w1c_wikievents_kappa_results.md
416	WikiEvents role-level tightness (Eq.~\ref{eq:tightness}, with $|\mathcal{E}|{=}27$) has mean $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.82$ and median 0.89; 84\% of roles have $\kappa \geq 0.80$ (4 or fewer valid entity types out of 27).
417	The tightness-violation pattern extends to event extraction: roles with $\kappa \geq 0.90$ exhibit the highest AC rate (50\%), compared to 21\% for medium-tightness ($0.80 \leq \kappa < 0.90$) and 30\% for loose ($\kappa < 0.80$) roles; this is consistent with the RE observation that constraint tightness correlates with violation frequency.
418	However, the correlation is modulated by entity type prototypicality: semantically intuitive roles (e.g., Transportation.Vehicle$\to$VEH) show 0\% AC despite $\kappa{=}0.963$, while roles that require rare KAIROS-specific types (e.g., Crime$\to$CRM, Sentence$\to$SEN) show near-100\% AC because LLMs default to common types (PER, ORG).
419	The loose group's elevated rate (30\%) is driven by high-frequency roles such as Attack.Target (126 args, 34\% AC, $\kappa{=}0.59$), where systematic model errors outweigh the permissive constraint space.
420

Dual Mechanism %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, llama_scierc_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1\n\n\\paragraph{CF Dual Mechanism.}\nCF violations arise from two distinct mechanisms:\n(1)~\\emph{Registry override}: the semantic constraint forces a domain-compatible type that conflicts with the entity's previously registered type.\nThe entity appears in multiple relations with different type requirements, and the per-relation mask selects the locally valid type without consulting prior assignments.\n(2)~\\emph{Grammar-first fallback} (\\S\\ref{sec:interaction}): when the semantic mask conflicts with all grammar-valid tokens, the semantic layer is dropped entirely, allowing the grammar layer to generate any structurally valid type, which may differ from the entity's registered type.\n\nThese mechanisms have sharply different model profiles.\nQwen3-8B triggers zero grammar-first fallbacks across all experiments, attributing 100\\% of its CF violations to registry override.\nLlama-3.1-8B, by contrast, triggers 5{,}882 fallbacks on SciERC (10.7 per document) and 57{,}825 on DocRED (63.8 per document), making grammar-first fallback the dominant CF source.\nThis asymmetry is consistent with the cross-model CF gap: Qwen achieves CF\\,=\\,2.57\\% on DocRED vs.\\ Llama's 12.02\\%, despite both using identical constraint configurations.\nThe difference traces to Llama's less calibrated type predictions, which more frequently conflict with the semantic mask's valid set, triggering fallback.\n\n%%% Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage %%%\n% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (final data)\n\n\\paragraph{Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage.}\n\\label{app:fallback-coverage}\nThe two-layer architecture produces two distinct fallback mechanisms with different semantic consequences.\n\\emph{Grammar-first fallback} (36.6\\% of type-bearing positions on SciERC) occurs when $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$: the semantic layer deactivates as a safety valve, preserving structural validity at the cost of potential type violations. These events account for the residual DR that persists after constraint enforcement.\n\\emph{Registry-consistent fallback} (4.5\\%) occurs when the entity's registered type conflicts with domain/range constraints; the system defaults to the registered type for cross-triple consistency.\nTogether, these mechanisms produce the characteristic profile of composable constraint systems: residual DR traces to grammar-first fallback events, while CF violations arise from registry override events where per-relation enforcement assigns conflicting types.\n\nWe define \\emph{effective semantic constraint coverage} as $1 - f_{\\text{gf}}$, where $f_{\\text{gf}}$ is the grammar-first fallback rate.\nOn SciERC, Llama-3.1-8B shows $f_{\\text{gf}} = 36.6\\%$ (6{,}237 of 17{,}024 mask calls), yielding 63.4\\% effective coverage.\nOn DocRED, by contrast, Qwen3-8B shows $f_{\\text{gf}} = 0.08\\%$ (46 of 54{,}859), yielding 99.9\\% coverage.\nThe large discrepancy is primarily a tokenization artifact: SciERC entity types are multi-token (e.g., \\textsc{OtherScientificTerm}, averaging 2.87 mask calls per type field), and continuation tokens necessarily conflict with the semantic mask, inflating the per-mask-call fallback rate.\nDocRED types (\\textsc{PER}, \\textsc{ORG}, \\textsc{LOC}, \\textsc{TIME}, \\textsc{NUM}, \\textsc{MISC}) are all single-token, eliminating this inflation.\nThis metric directly quantifies the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:interaction}): it measures the fraction of decoding steps where both constraint layers are jointly active, providing a fine-grained view beyond aggregate violation rates.\nQwen3-8B, with zero grammar-first fallbacks, achieves 100\\% effective coverage on SciERC, consistent with its lower residual DR (0.93\\% vs.\\ Llama's 0.08\\%).\n\n%%% CF-DR Trade-off Analysis %%%\n% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (CF fallback vs full_s1 baseline)\n\n\\paragraph{CF-DR Trade-off Analysis.}\n\\label{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}\n\nCF violations are the most prominent residual limitation of OntoCoDec under zero-shot decoding (9.90\\% on SciERC, up to 12.02\\% on Llama DocRED).\nWe treat this honestly: per-relation independent enforcement cannot eliminate CF without trading it against DR.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff} reports a pilot ablation in which we replace the grammar-first fallback with a CF-priority fallback that forces the entity's registered type when it conflicts with $\\sigma(r)$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Configuration} & \\textbf{DR\\,(\\%)} & \\textbf{CF\\,(\\%)} & \\textbf{DR+CF} & \\textbf{$f_{\\text{gf}}$\\,(\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull S1 (baseline) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 10.83 & --- \\\\\nCF fallback        & 14.78 & 0.04 & 14.82 & 36.64 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$ & $+$13.85 & $-$9.86 & $+$3.99 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{CF-DR trade-off pilot on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents; \\texttt{w1\\_cf\\_fallback\\_scierc\\_pilot}). CF fallback eliminates CF but re-routes violations to DR, increasing total violations (DR+CF) by 3.99\\,pp. $f_{\\text{gf}}$: grammar-first fallback rate at type-bearing positions.}\n\\label{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe CF fallback variant nearly eliminates CF (9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.04\\%, $-$9.86\\,pp) but at the cost of a 15.9$\\times$ increase in DR (0.93\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.78\\%, $+$13.85\\,pp).\n% exp_id: w1_cf_fallback_scierc_pilot: DR=0.1478, CF=0.0004, f_gf=0.3664\nThis is not a net improvement: total violations (DR+CF) rise from 10.83\\% to 14.82\\% ($+$3.99\\,pp), so the change \\emph{re-routes} violations rather than reducing them.\nThe mechanism is mechanical: each token position with conflicting masks must commit to one violation type; when the CF fallback forces the system to emit the entity's registered type even though it lies outside $\\sigma(r)$, the resulting triple satisfies cross-entity consistency but violates domain/range.\nThe grammar-first fallback is triggered at 36.64\\% of type-bearing positions; this conflict is structural, not a tunable hyperparameter.\n\nWe interpret this as a fundamental tension in composable constraint systems with \\emph{per-relation independent} enforcement: enforcement priority can be allocated between DR and CF but cannot independently minimize both.\nWithin this design, the current full S1 configuration sits near the Pareto frontier of the trade-off, achieving 0.93\\% DR at the cost of 9.90\\% CF.\nThe direction of the trade-off is consistent beyond SciERC/Qwen3-8B: across all six zero-shot configurations (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets), DR and CF are inversely associated (Spearman $\\rho = -0.77$, $n{=}6$), with no setting showing both high DR and high CF simultaneously (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}).\nThe pattern is a structural consequence of per-relation enforcement with grammar-first fallback, not a model- or dataset-specific artifact; we select SciERC/Qwen3-8B for detailed ablation because it exhibits the highest CF (9.90\\%), making the trade-off most visible.\nWhile the inverse association holds across all configurations, the absolute trade-off magnitude may differ in settings with higher baseline CF, such as Llama DocRED (CF = 12.02\\%), where the larger CF headroom could yield proportionally larger shifts under alternative fallback priorities.\nThe CF residual is concentrated in schema ambiguity rather than enforcement failure: 63.3\\% of CF violations on SciERC involve the catch-all \\textsc{Generic} type (Table~\\ref{tab:cf-distribution}), which competes with every content type depending on anaphoric vs.\\ referential use, a distinction the per-slot decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.\n\nTwo complementary paths can lower CF without re-routing it to DR.\n(i)~\\emph{Reduce the baseline conflict rate.} Composing OntoCoDec with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC reduces CF by 88\\% relative to the zero-shot setting (9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.18\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:composability}), because the fine-tuned model's per-slot type predictions agree more often with the entity registry, so the per-relation mask and the registry intersect more frequently.\n% exp_id: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval: CF=0.0118\nThe remaining 1.18\\% still traces to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity, so this path is empirically validated on one dataset and one fine-tuning method but does not generalize without further evidence.\n(ii)~\\emph{Replace per-relation enforcement with global entity-level type consensus during decoding.} This is outside the scope of OntoCoDec's forward-only autoregressive design and is left to future work.\n\n%%% Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1,\n%          llama_scierc_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1\n\n\\paragraph{Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis.}\n\\label{app:dual-factor}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:dual-factor-scatter} plots residual DR against the product $\\bar{\\kappa} \\times \\text{RV}_{\\text{baseline}}$ for model$\\times$dataset configurations.\nFor \\emph{calibrated} models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B; unconstrained ET${<}$3\\% on both datasets), a linear fit yields $y = 0.366x - 0.138$ with $R^2 = 0.886$ ($n{=}4$, $F(1,2) = 15.54$, $p \\approx 0.058$); with only four data points (df $= 2$) and $p > 0.05$, this constitutes a descriptive characterization rather than a confirmatory statistical test.\nThe strong fit supports the dual-factor pattern from \\S\\ref{sec:composition}: residual DR is governed by the product of schema tightness and baseline violation rate.\nMistral-7B (unconstrained ET${=}$7.58\\% on DocRED) deviates from this pattern: its residual DR on DocRED (33.21\\%) substantially exceeds the linear prediction. When model calibration is insufficient (ET${>}$5\\% on either dataset), residual DR is dominated by entity misclassification rather than constraint-search failure.\nThis motivates a principled exclusion criterion: models with unconstrained ET${>}$5\\% on either dataset fall outside the regime where the tightness$\\times$RV product is the primary DR driver.\nThe two DocRED points (high $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$) separate cleanly from the two SciERC points (low $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), and within each dataset the model with higher baseline RV shows higher residual DR.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/dual_factor_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Residual DR vs.\\ $\\bar{\\kappa} \\times \\text{RV}_{\\text{baseline}}$ across four model$\\times$dataset configurations.\nLinear fit (calibrated models): $y = 0.366x - 0.138$, $R^2 = 0.886$; Mistral deviates due to entity misclassification.}\n\\label{fig:dual-factor-scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%%% Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact (moved from §7) %%%\n% exp_ids: d012_bootstrap_ci (DocRED +0.50pp p<0.001, SciERC +0.13pp p=0.58),\n%          phase0_docred_unconstrained (RV=29.66%), phase0_scierc_unconstrained (RV=1.39%)\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact.}\nThe divergence in F1 significance between DocRED ($+$0.50\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) and SciERC ($+$0.13\\,pp, $p = 0.58$) traces to a single mechanism: grammar-layer elimination of out-of-vocabulary relation names.\nDocRED's baseline RV of 29.66\\% produces 5{,}189 OOV predictions counted as false positives; eliminating them yields a significant precision gain.\nSciERC's baseline RV is only 1.39\\% (24 OOV predictions), too few to produce a detectable F1 effect.\nLlama-3.1-8B's lower baseline RV (16.03\\%) yields a smaller but directionally consistent improvement ($+$0.29\\,pp vs.\\ $+$0.50\\,pp); F1 gain magnitude is proportional to baseline OOV rate.\n% exp_ids: phase0_docred_unconstrained, phase0_scierc_unconstrained\n% exp_ids: llama_docred_unconstrained (RV=16.03%), llama_docred_full_s1 (R-F1 delta=+0.29pp)\n\n%%% Paragraph 1: Low F1 Practical Significance %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%), phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%),\n%          phase0_scierc_unconstrained (DR=15.37%), phase2_docred_unconstrained (DR=18.46%)\n\n\\paragraph{Practical Significance of Zero-Shot F1.}\nOur reported relation F1 of approximately 8\\% on both SciERC and DocRED reflects the inherent difficulty of zero-shot closed IE, where the model receives no task-specific training examples.\nFor reference, supervised state-of-the-art systems on SciERC achieve approximately 49--53\\% relation F1 with full training data~\\citep{zhong2021frustratingly,ye2022packed}.\nConstraint enforcement operates largely independently of the extraction model's intrinsic capability: it restricts the output space to ontologically valid triples regardless of the underlying model's accuracy.\nEven after LoRA fine-tuning, residual DR of 1--2\\% persists on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); constraints remain necessary at deployment-grade quality.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval\nIn our evaluation, F1 serves as a \\emph{control variable} verifying that constraint enforcement does not degrade extraction quality, not as an optimization target.\nThe contribution of this work is the constraint enforcement method itself; the zero-shot setting stress-tests constraint robustness under maximum model uncertainty, while composability with fine-tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}) validates practical deployment value.\n\n%%% Paragraph 4: Decoding-Time vs. Post-Hoc Enforcement %%%\n% exp_ids: posthoc_filtering_baseline, phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%),\n%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%, ET=0%)\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding-Time vs.\\ Post-Hoc Enforcement.}\nPost-hoc filtering (generating triples without constraints, then removing schema-violating predictions) yields higher precision and consequently higher F1 than OntoCoDec on both datasets (SciERC 8.63 vs.\\ 8.36; DocRED 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23).\n% posthoc: SciERC F1=8.63, DocRED F1=9.61; OntoCoDec: SciERC R-F1=8.36, DocRED R-F1=8.23\nHowever, this precision gain comes at substantial cost.\nOn DocRED, post-hoc filtering discards 48.1\\% of all predicted triples, including 127 true positives (11.2\\% of correct predictions); recall drops from 9.46\\% to 8.40\\%.\n% posthoc_docred: rejection=48.1%, tp_lost=127/1137, R: 9.46→8.40\nEntity type violations remain unresolved (ET\\,=\\,2.47\\% vs.\\ OntoCoDec's 0\\%), as filtering targets only domain/range and vocabulary violations.\n% posthoc_docred: ET_residual=2.47%; phase2_docred_full_s1: ET=0%\nOntoCoDec's decoding-time approach avoids this information loss by guiding generation toward compliant outputs rather than discarding non-compliant ones, preserving both recall and entity type validity.\nThe two strategies are complementary: post-hoc filtering can serve as an additional safety net for residual violations that decoding-time enforcement does not fully resolve.\n\n%%% CF Violation Distribution %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (CF=245), llama_scierc_full_s1 (CF=162),\n%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (CF=2670), llama_docred_full_s1 (CF=3824)\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Field Violation Distribution.}\n\\label{app:cf-distribution}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cf-distribution} reports the complete distribution of conflicting-type pairs for SciERC full S1 (245 CF violations, all pairwise; no mention triggered $\\geq$3 types).\nConflicts involving the \\textsc{Generic} type account for 63.3\\% (155/245) of all violations.\n\\textsc{Generic} is SciERC's anaphoric/placeholder label (``this approach'', ``the method''), which competes with every content type depending on whether the mention is used anaphorically or referentially, a distinction the per-slot constrained decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.\n\nThe pattern generalizes across models and datasets: on Llama-3.1-8B SciERC, \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} remains the top pair (53/162, 32.7\\%); on DocRED, the analogous catch-all type \\textsc{MISC} drives 76.6\\% of Qwen's CF violations.\nUnder unconstrained decoding, CF rates collapse to $\\leq$1\\% on both datasets; CF violations are a side-effect of per-slot type commitment rather than a model deficiency.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lr lora_scierc_full_s1\n\n\\paragraph{Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning.}\n\\label{app:lora-per-relation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:lora-per-relation} presents the per-relation breakdown of DR
