defense, providing 39--72\\% reduction.\nF1 cost is negligible: max $\\Delta$R-F1 = $-$0.30\\,pp (Qwen DocRED full vs.\\ unconstrained).}\n\\label{tab:ft-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe grammar layer is effectively a no-op for fine-tuned models across all four settings: RV and ET are already 0\\% without constraints, and grammar-only DR change is $<$0.2\\,pp (4/4 settings).\nThe semantic layer is the sole effective DR defense under fine-tuning, independently reducing DR by 39--72\\% across all settings (SciERC: Qwen $-$69\\%, Llama $-$41\\%; DocRED: Qwen $-$72\\%, Llama $-$39\\%).\nAdding both layers achieves near-complete elimination (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% in all 4 settings): even after fine-tuning, the semantic layer's pair-based enforcement captures violations that training alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.\nF1 cost is negligible: the maximum R-F1 decrease across all 16 model$\\times$dataset$\\times$mode combinations is $-$0.30\\,pp (Qwen DocRED, full vs.\\ unconstrained), attributable to parse failure increase (7.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,8.52\\%) rather than constraint-induced extraction degradation.\n\n%%% Residual DR Error Analysis (Fine-Tuned) %%%\n% exp_ids: w5_qwen_docred_*, w5_llama_docred_*, w5_lora_scierc_*, w5_llama_lora_scierc_*\n\n\\paragraph{Residual DR Error Analysis.}\n\\label{app:residual-dr-analysis}\n\nAcross all fine-tuned settings (2 models $\\times$ 2 datasets $\\times$ full S1), only 36 domain/range violations remain.\nManual inspection reveals four root causes:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\emph{Entity type errors} (39\\%): the LLM assigns an incorrect entity type (e.g., labeling a geographic entity as \\textsc{LOC} when the gold standard requires \\textsc{MISC}), causing the semantic mask to permit a domain/range-incompatible relation.\n\\item \\emph{Schema coverage gaps} (33\\%): the frequency-based constraint table, constructed from training-set co-occurrence statistics, does not cover long-tail type pairs that are ontologically valid but rare. These violations are irreducible by decoding-time constraints alone.\n\\item \\emph{Incorrect relation selection} (17\\%): the model selects a semantically inappropriate relation whose domain/range constraints are incompatible with the correctly typed entities.\n\\item \\emph{Direction reversal} (11\\%): the model assigns the correct relation but inverts head/tail roles, placing entities in positions that violate the relation's asymmetric domain/range specification.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nTwo dataset-specific patterns emerge.\nOutside Llama DocRED, all residual violations (3 of 36, spanning Llama SciERC and Qwen DocRED) trace exclusively to schema coverage gaps: the model's entity typing is accurate, and the constraint system has reached its theoretical ceiling given the frequency-based constraint table.\nOn DocRED, Llama-3.1-8B contributes 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), with entity type errors as the dominant cause (14/33, 42\\%).\nThis asymmetry reflects Llama's weaker fine-tuned entity typing on DocRED's larger ontology (96 relations, 7 entity types) compared to Qwen, which achieves near-zero residual.\nThe implication is twofold: (i)~the irreducible floor from schema gaps is small ($\\sim$3 violations across 4 settings), so the constraint system operates near its theoretical optimum; (ii)~residual violations from entity typing errors are addressable through stronger base models or improved entity typing modules, without modifying the constraint architecture.\n\n%%% Post-Hoc Filtering Comparison (Extended) %%%\n% exp_ids: posthoc_*_zs, posthoc_*_ft\n\n\\paragraph{Post-Hoc Filtering: Extended Comparison.}\n\\label{app:posthoc-extended}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:posthoc-extended} presents the full comparison across all six zero-shot and four fine-tuned configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrr l@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Reject\\%} & \\textbf{R-F1$_{\\text{ph}}$} & \\textbf{R-F1$_{\\text{cd}}$} & \\textbf{Winner} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{ZS}\n  & Qwen    & SciERC & 16.8 & 8.63  & 8.36  & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & SciERC & 13.9 & 2.71  & 2.54  & ph \\\\\n  & Mistral & SciERC & 20.3 & 3.94  & 3.67  & ph \\\\\n  & Qwen    & DocRED & 48.1 & 9.61  & 8.23  & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & DocRED & 42.2 & 7.44  & 6.27  & ph \\\\\n  & Mistral & DocRED & \\textbf{73.7} & 3.85  & \\textbf{5.52} & cd \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{FT}\n  & Qwen    & SciERC & 1.95 & 43.94 & 43.77 & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & SciERC & 1.11 & 45.23 & 45.47 & cd \\\\\n  & Qwen    & DocRED & 1.03 & 41.11 & 40.56 & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & DocRED & 0.64 & 43.92 & 43.78 & cd \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Post-hoc filtering (ph) vs.\\ constrained decoding (cd).\nIn zero-shot settings, post-hoc F1 is higher in 5/6 groups (precision-dominated), but constrained decoding preserves higher recall in all 6/6 groups.\nAt reject rate $>$70\\% (Mistral DocRED), post-hoc F1 collapses below constrained.\nUnder fine-tuning, rejection rates drop to 0.6--2.0\\% and the two strategies are equivalent ($\\Delta \\leq 0.55$\\,pp; each wins two groups).\nF1 $\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:posthoc-extended}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe crossover point occurs when post-hoc rejection exceeds $\\sim$70\\%: Mistral DocRED rejects 73.7\\% of predicted triples, discarding enough true positives that constrained decoding's recall advantage dominates ($+$1.67\\,pp).\nOntoCoDec's advantage thus scales with violation severity: precisely when constraints are most needed, post-hoc filtering is most destructive.\nUnder fine-tuning, both strategies converge because few violations remain to filter or constrain ($\\Delta \\leq 0.55$\\,pp across all four FT groups, with each strategy winning two).\n\n%%% Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling %%%\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling.}\n\\label{app:runtime}\n\nOn SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot greedy, NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition), unconstrained inference averages 4.163\\,s/doc and Full S1 constrained decoding averages 4.710\\,s/doc ($+$13.1\\% overhead).\nOn DocRED (same configuration), unconstrained inference averages 19.34\\,s/doc and Full S1 averages 19.81\\,s/doc ($+$2.43\\% overhead).\nThe lower relative overhead on DocRED reflects amortization: the fixed per-token constraint cost is spread over longer generation sequences.\nXGrammar automaton compilation takes 0.044\\,s; the full constraint pipeline (schema parsing, JSON Schema generation, grammar compilation) takes 0.33\\,s on SciERC (Table~\\ref{tab:scalability}), amortized to zero over the evaluation set.\n\n% exp_id: p8c_ft_scierc_latency — Reviewer Report #23 verified\n\\paragraph{Fine-Tuned Latency Profiling.}\n\\label{app:ft-latency}\nUnder LoRA fine-tuning (same hardware and dataset), unconstrained inference averages 2.612\\,s/doc and Full S1 constrained decoding averages 2.873\\,s/doc ($+$10.0\\% overhead, $n{=}551$).\nFine-tuned models generate shorter, more structured outputs than zero-shot models (2.612\\,s vs.\\ 4.163\\,s/doc unconstrained); absolute generation time drops by 37\\%.\nThe relative overhead ($+$10.0\\%) is slightly lower than zero-shot ($+$13.1\\%), consistent with shorter sequences requiring fewer constraint checks per document.\n\n%%% Fuzzy Relation Matching %%%\n\n\\paragraph{Relation Matching Protocol.}\n\\label{app:fuzzy-matching}\nRelation F1 evaluation uses fuzzy normalization to handle model-specific formatting variants (e.g., underscores vs.\\ spaces, abbreviations).\nA predicted relation $r_p$ matches gold relation $r_g$ if: (1)~exact string match after case-insensitive normalization, or (2)~token overlap $>$80\\% \\emph{and} edit distance $\\leq 2$.\nThis protocol affects primarily Mistral, which produces underscore-separated variants (e.g., ``head\\_of\\_government'' for ``head of government'').\nOn Mistral DocRED, fuzzy matching contributes 98 additional TPs ($+$0.84\\,pp R-F1; strict R-F1\\,=\\,3.50\\% vs.\\ fuzzy 4.34\\%).\nFor Qwen and Llama under grammar constraints, the grammar layer enforces exact relation names, making fuzzy matching irrelevant (0 additional matches).\nAll F1 figures in the main text use fuzzy normalization consistently across all models and conditions.\n\n%%% Per-Relation Violation Landscape %%%\n\n\\paragraph{Per-Relation Violation Landscape.}\n\\label{app:per-relation-landscape}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:prompt_comparison} compares violation rates under unconstrained and schema-informed prompting.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per_relation_dr} shows the per-relation DR distribution on SciERC.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under unconstrained and schema-informed prompting (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).}\n\\label{tab:prompt_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & Prompt & DR$\\downarrow$ & RV$\\downarrow$ & ET$\\downarrow$ & CF$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC} & Unconstrained & 15.4 & 1.4 & 0.4 & 0.1 \\\\\n & Schema-informed & 5.8 & 0.3 & \\textbf{0.0} & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED} & Unconstrained & 18.5 & 29.7 & 2.5 & 0.1 \\\\\n & Schema-informed & 12.8 & 18.4 & 0.2 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_per_relation_dr.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-relation DR violation rates on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, unconstrained). Rates correlate with constraint tightness $\\kappa(r)$.}\n\\label{fig:per_relation_dr}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%%% WikiEvents Extended %%%\n\n\\paragraph{WikiEvents Dataset Details.}\n\\label{app:wikievents}\nWikiEvents~\\cite{li2021document} is a document-level event argument extraction benchmark grounded in the DARPA KAIROS ontology.\nIt defines 56 event types, 80 argument roles, and 27 entity types, with 245 (event\\_type, role)$\\to$allowed\\_entity\\_types constraints.\nThe grammar layer targets EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and ET; the semantic layer enforces AC (argument constraint) via role-conditioned masking, analogous to RE domain/range enforcement.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness in WikiEvents.}\n\\label{app:wikievents-kappa}\n% Data source: artifacts/w1c_wikievents_kappa_results.md\nWikiEvents role-level tightness (Eq.~\\ref{eq:tightness}, with $|\\mathcal{E}|{=}27$) has mean $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.82$ and median 0.89; 84\\% of roles have $\\kappa \\geq 0.80$ (4 or fewer valid entity types out of 27).\nThe tightness-violation pattern extends to event extraction: roles with $\\kappa \\geq 0.90$ exhibit the highest AC rate (50\\%), compared to 21\\% for medium-tightness ($0.80 \\leq \\kappa < 0.90$) and 30\\% for loose ($\\kappa < 0.80$) roles; this is consistent with the RE observation that constraint tightness correlates with violation frequency.\nHowever, the correlation is modulated by entity type prototypicality: semantically intuitive roles (e.g., Transportation.Vehicle$\\to$VEH) show 0\\% AC despite $\\kappa{=}0.963$, while roles that require rare KAIROS-specific types (e.g., Crime$\\to$CRM, Sentence$\\to$SEN) show near-100\\% AC because LLMs default to common types (PER, ORG).\nThe loose group's elevated rate (30\\%) is driven by high-frequency roles such as Attack.Target (126 args, 34\\% AC, $\\kappa{=}0.59$), where systematic model errors outweigh the permissive constraint space.\n",

55	F1 $\times$100. Bootstrap significance: R-F1 $+$0.50\,pp ($p < 0.001$).
56	Grammar-only $\uparrow$DR: vocabulary enforcement makes OOV outputs evaluable, exposing latent violations (see text).}
57	\label{tab:docred-ablation}
58	\end{table}
59	
60	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_full_s1
61	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
62	Full S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates ET ($\to$\,0\%), and near-eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).\footnote{The residual 0.03\% RV traces to a rare XGrammar automaton edge case where subword tokens from different enum entries combine into chimeric values (e.g., \texttt{member} + \texttt{icipant\_of} from \texttt{member\_of} and \texttt{participant\_of}). This is not a fundamental limitation of the approach.}
63	R-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.
64	CF increases modestly (0.10\%\,$\to$\,2.57\%), consistent with the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
65	
66	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only, phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
67	\paragraph{Layer contributions.}
68	Grammar-only eliminates RV and ET but \emph{increases} DR to 26.17\%: OOV triples (29.66\% baseline) become evaluable and expose latent type incompatibilities.\footnote{The grammar-only DR increase reflects constraint-altered generation trajectories in long documents, where vocabulary restriction shifts token probabilities away from natural but type-violating sequences.}
69	Semantic-only reduces DR by 25\% but cannot eliminate RV or ET, the same enabling dependency observed on SciERC.
70	Residual DR (10.21\% vs.\ SciERC's 0.93\%) reflects higher tightness ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$ vs.\ 0.333), which forces frequent fallback; effective semantic coverage is $\sim$90\% (Appendix~\ref{app:fallback-coverage}).
71	Under fine-tuning, residual narrows to 0.01--0.21\% (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
72	The increase in Llama parse failures under constraints (6.8\%\,$\to$\,9.1\%) reflects an inherent trade-off: constraint masking alters token probability distributions, which can push generation toward suboptimal paths in long documents.
73

136	Llama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\%, RV=0\%, DR=0\%, CF=0\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.
137	% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc
138	The three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.
139	
140	Beam search on DocRED (zero-shot, 50 documents) introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\%) compared to greedy (1.1\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.
141	With DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\% CI upper bound is 5.8\%; the full-scale LoRA evaluation below (998 documents, CI${\approx}$0.3\%) provides stronger statistical confirmation.
142	
143	Table~\ref{tab:beam-docred-lora} reports LoRA + beam-4 results on the full DocRED dev set (998 documents), extending the fine-tuning composability evaluation from SciERC to the higher-$\bar{\kappa}$ regime.
144	Parse failure rates under LoRA beam-4 range from 1.3\% to 3.5\%, lower than the zero-shot rate.
```

273	The grammar layer is effectively a no-op for fine-tuned models across all four settings: RV and ET are already 0\% without constraints, and grammar-only DR change is $<$0.2\,pp (4/4 settings).
274	The semantic layer is the sole effective DR defense under fine-tuning, independently reducing DR by 39--72\% across all settings (SciERC: Qwen $-$69\%, Llama $-$41\%; DocRED: Qwen $-$72\%, Llama $-$39\%).
275	Adding both layers achieves near-complete elimination (DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\% in all 4 settings): even after fine-tuning, the semantic layer's pair-based enforcement captures violations that training alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.
276	F1 cost is negligible: the maximum R-F1 decrease across all 16 model$\times$dataset$\times$mode combinations is $-$0.30\,pp (Qwen DocRED, full vs.\ unconstrained), attributable to parse failure increase (7.11\%\,$\to$\,8.52\%) rather than constraint-induced extraction degradation.

R-F1 changes are $-$0.30\\,pp and $+$0.06\\,pp.\nThe lower Llama-DocRED reduction (57\\% vs.\\ 92--100\\%) traces to higher tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), which triggers grammar-first fallback more frequently.\nAcross all four model-dataset settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.76 & 0.09 & 40.86 & 82.15 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.01} & 0.27 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nDR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% across both datasets with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nF1\\,$\\times$100. only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08  & 15.45\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08  & 14.92\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71  & 14.36\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90  & \\textbf{10.83}\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{9.00}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nTot.\\ = DR + CF (total type-violation burden).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded; Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.\nS1 = single-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}), S2 = two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\nGrammar-only represents the ceiling of grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}: vocabulary membership is enforced but cross-field semantic constraints are not.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations that vary by schema tightness (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nOn SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), adding S1 eliminates or near-eliminates DR: Qwen 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nOn DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 96 relations), S1 reduces DR from 0.76\\% to 0.01\\% (Qwen, $\\downarrow$99\\%) and 0.49\\% to 0.21\\% (Llama, $\\downarrow$57\\%); R-F1 changes are $-$0.30\\,pp and $+$0.06\\,pp.\nThe lower Llama-DocRED reduction (57\\% vs.\\ 92--100\\%) traces to higher tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), which triggers grammar-first fallback more frequently.\nAcross all four model-dataset settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); the semantic layer alone accounts for 39--72\\% of DR reduction under fine-tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 is the recommended fast deployment configuration: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\% at R-F1 40--46, with $+$10\\% latency overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:runtime}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both Qwen and Llama (R-F1 44.23, 46.11; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.56 & 0.08 & 40.89 & 82.35 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.01} & 0.27 & 40.56 & 81.43 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 43.72 & 83.93 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nDR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% across both datasets with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nF1\\,$\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations. ---: not computed.\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--9\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%); excluding parse failures, E-F1 is stable for both models ($\\pm$0.1\\,pp).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",

grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations that vary by schema tightness (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nOn SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), adding S1 eliminates or near-eliminates DR: Qwen 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nOn DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 96 relations), S1 reduces DR from 0.76\\% to 0.01\\% (Qwen, $\\downarrow$99\\%) and 0.49\\% to 0.21\\% (Llama, $\\downarrow$57\\%); R-F1 changes are $-$0.30\\,pp and $+$0.06\\,pp.\nThe lower Llama-DocRED reduction (57\\% vs.\\ 92--100\\%) traces to higher tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), which triggers grammar-first fallback more frequently.\nAcross all four model-dataset settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.56 & 0.08 & 40.89 & 82.35 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.22 & 40.79 & 81.87 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 44.55 & 84.77 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.21 & 0.21 & 43.78 & 83.94 & $\\downarrow$57\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nDR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% across both datasets with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nF1\\,$\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.\nS1 = single-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}), S2 = two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\nGrammar-only represents the ceiling of grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}: vocabulary membership is enforced but cross-field semantic constraints are not.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations that vary by schema tightness (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nOn SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), adding S1 eliminates or near-eliminates DR: Qwen 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nOn DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 96 relations), S1 reduces DR from 0.76\\% to 0.01\\% (Qwen, $\\downarrow$99\\%) and 0.49\\% to 0.21\\% (Llama, $\\downarrow$57\\%); R-F1 changes are $-$0.30\\,pp and $+$0.06\\,pp.\nThe lower Llama-DocRED reduction (57\\% vs.\\ 92--100\\%) traces to higher tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), which triggers grammar-first fallback more frequently.\nAcross all four model-dataset settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); the semantic layer alone accounts for 39--72\\% of DR reduction under fine-tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 is the recommended fast deployment configuration: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\% at R-F1 40--46, with $+$10\\% latency overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:runtime}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both Qwen and Llama (R-F1 44.23, 46.11;     "originalFile": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-7} \\cmidrule(l){8-9}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF & Tot.\\,$\\downarrow$\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08  & 15.45\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08  & 14.92\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71  & 14.36\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90  & \\textbf{10.83}\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{9.00}\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nTot.\\ = DR + CF (total type-violation burden).\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded; Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.\nS1 = single-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}), S2 = two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\nGrammar-only represents the ceiling of grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}: vocabulary membership is enforced but cross-field semantic constraints are not.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations that vary by schema tightness (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nOn SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), adding S1 eliminates DR: Qwen 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nOn DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 96 relations), S1 eliminates DR: Qwen 0.56\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% and Llama 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; R-F1 changes are $-$0.10\\,pp and $+$0.15\\,pp.\nAcross all four model-dataset settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot SciERC) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); the semantic layer alone accounts for 39--72\\% of DR reduction under fine-tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 is the recommended fast deployment configuration: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\% at R-F1 40--46, with $+$10\\% latency overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:runtime}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both Qwen and Llama (R-F1 44.23, 46.11; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.56 & 0.08 & 40.89 & 82.35 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.22 & 40.79 & 81.87 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 44.55 & 84.77 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.00 & 0.26 & 44.70 & 84.71 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nDR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% across both datasets with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nF1\\,$\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations. ---: not computed.\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--9\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%); excluding parse failures, E-F1 is stable for both models ($\\pm$0.1\\,pp).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "newString": "eliminates DR violations (DR $\\leq$ 0.09\\%)",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) guarantees JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nWithout constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \\emph{constraint tightness} ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) rather than schema or model scale.\nThe two layers exhibit a formal enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR violations (DR $\\leq$ 0.21\\%) with negligible R-F1 impact ($\\pm$0.6\\,pp); beam search ($k{=}4$) resolves the cross-field consistency (CF) trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (R-F1 44--46, 1.54$\\times$ latency).\nUnder zero-shot decoding, DR drops by 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\nThe framework extends to event extraction without modification, validating the ontology-agnostic compilation design.\n\\textsc{OntoCoDec} compiles constraints automatically from any IE ontology at inference time, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM across four models (8B--70B) and three tokenizer families.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

  "tool_use_result": {

    "newString": "eliminates DR violations (DR $\\leq$ 0.09\\%)",
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity is guaranteed to carry a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 1--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with \\emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nType violations are ontologically invalid regardless of downstream application: a triple whose head or tail type lies outside the relation's domain/range specification contradicts the schema definition.\nAt deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption; reliable type-safety enforcement is needed.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} guarantees syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives, sacrificing recall; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\\%), it becomes impractical.\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime guarantees.\nThese two directions—grammar-constrained decoding and schema-aware training—have developed independently; no method provides both semantic awareness and runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that formally compiles IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} enforces context-free constraints (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} enforces prefix-decidable constraints (pair-based domain/range) through dynamic logit masking over a runtime entity registry, with an \\emph{enabling dependency} between layers.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from any IE ontology, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM.\nWe evaluate two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($\\leq$ 0.21\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp and $+$10\\% latency; LoRA + beam S1 ($k{=}4$) additionally resolves the CF trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nZero-shot evaluation across four models (8B--70B) confirms DR drops by 76--99\\% for calibrated models.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A formal ontology-to-constraint compilation framework: we classify IE type constraints by decidability (context-free, prefix-decidable with state, non-local), yielding a two-layer architecture with pair-based sequential enforcement and formal guarantees at each level (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from any IE ontology.\n    \\item An analytical framework linking constraint tightness to enforcement outcomes: $\\bar{\\kappa}$ characterizes dataset-level DR reduction (94--99\\% at $\\bar{\\kappa}{<}0.5$ vs.\\ 42--90\\% at $\\bar{\\kappa}{>}0.8$), with entity type confusability as the critical co-factor; $\\bar{\\kappa}$ additionally determines post-hoc resolution feasibility (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n    \\item Empirical validation of the enabling dependency and composability: the five-way ablation confirms that neither layer alone achieves $>$24\\% DR reduction, while their composition yields 94--100\\%; fine-tuning does not obviate constraints (residual DR 1--2\\% at R-F1 40--46\\%); the framework generalizes to event extraction with 245 constraints across three tokenizer families (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          " OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from any IE ontology, requires no constraint-specific training, and applies to any autoregressive LM.",
          "-We evaluate two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($\\leq$ 0.21\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp and $+$10\\% latency; LoRA + beam S1 ($k{=}4$) additionally resolves the CF trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency).",
          "+We evaluate two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 eliminates DR violations (DR $\\leq$ 0.09\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp and $+$10\\% latency; LoRA + beam S1 ($k{=}4$) additionally resolves the CF trade-off, achieving zero violations on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency).",

    "originalFile": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\nWe evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations.\nLoRA + greedy S1 (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}) is the recommended fast configuration, near-eliminating DR violations with minimal latency overhead.\nLoRA + beam S1 (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}) additionally resolves cross-field consistency, achieving zero total violations at 1.54$\\times$ latency.\nZero-shot evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) isolates layer contributions as a diagnostic; R-F1 2--8 reflects the absence of task-specific training, not method failure.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nRE type constraints are extracted automatically from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) are derived from the KAIROS ontology.\nThe framework handles both sources without modification.\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nWe measure four violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), and \\textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR violations are 15.37\\% (SciERC) and 18.46\\% (DocRED), comparable rates associated with constraint tightness rather than ontology scale (\\S\\ref{sec:introduction}); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "1f44b160-d4f0-445c-8517-2a0734950462",

  "tool_use_result": {

    "oldString": "(DR\\,0.01--0.21\\%)",
    "newString": "(DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%)",
    "originalFile": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.\nAmong type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\nRecall the tightness of relation $r$ (\\S\\ref{sec:bg:ontology}):\\footnote{For symmetric relations, $|\\sigma(r)|$ counts ordered pairs; the normalization $|\\mathcal{E}|^2$ remains appropriate since we evaluate on directed triples.}\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:tightness}\n  \\kappa(r) = 1 - \\frac{|\\sigma(r)|}{|\\mathcal{E}|^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nThe mean tightness $\\bar{\\kappa} = \\frac{1}{|\\mathcal{R}|}\\sum_{r} \\kappa(r)$ characterizes schema restrictiveness.\nTighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions; fallback frequency and residual DR increase accordingly.\nThis dual-factor pattern (tightness $\\times$ baseline violation rate; $n{=}4$ model--dataset pairs, descriptive) aligns with the cross-dataset results: SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\\% DR reduction vs.\\ DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 45--90\\%; the full range across three models is 42--99\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dual-factor}).\nThe pattern extends to WikiEvents EE ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.82$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wikievents-kappa}).\nHowever, $\\kappa$ is necessary but not sufficient for high violation rates: per-relation analysis shows that high-$\\kappa$ relations with unambiguous entity types (\\textsc{place\\_of\\_birth}, $\\kappa{=}0.92$, DR${=}$0\\%) have zero violations, while relations with comparable $\\kappa$ but confusable types (\\textsc{basin\\_country}, $\\kappa{=}0.97$, DR${=}$74.6\\%) are highly vulnerable (Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa-case-study}).\n$\\bar{\\kappa}$ provides deployment guidance: schemas with $\\bar{\\kappa} < 0.5$ achieve near-complete DR elimination under zero-shot S1 (94--99\\% reduction); schemas with $\\bar{\\kappa} > 0.8$ require fine-tuning to narrow the fallback regime (DR\\,0.01--0.21\\%).\n$\\bar{\\kappa}$ also determines post-hoc resolution feasibility: low-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ schemas ($<$0.5) benefit from TCR with negligible DR regression ($\\leq$0.28\\,pp), while high-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ schemas ($>$0.8) suffer harmful DR increases (up to $+$9.38\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}\n\\label{sec:rectangularity}\n% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md\nSequential masking is necessary because ontology type signatures are non-rectangular (0/7 SciERC, 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\\sigma(r) = \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$); independent enforcement would admit 33--37\\% spurious pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nFine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% and CF to $\\leq$\\,1.18\\%, with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp; the grammar layer becomes a no-op and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.\nAmong type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\nRecall the tightness of relation $r$ (\\S\\ref{sec:bg:ontology}):\\footnote{For symmetric relations, $|\\sigma(r)|$ counts ordered pairs;     "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage[table]{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage{pifont}\n\\newcommand{\\TBD}[1]{\\textcolor{red}{#1}}\n\n\\title{OntoCoDec: Type-Constrained Information Extraction\\\\via Compiled Schema Constraints}\n\n\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n% === Abstract ===\n\\input{abstract}\n\n% === §1 Introduction ===\n\\input{section1_introduction_draft}\n\n% === §2 Background ===\n\\input{section2_background}\n\n% === §3 Method ===\n\\input{section3_method_draft}\n\n% === §4 Constraint Composition ===\n\\section{Constraint Composition}\n\\label{sec:composition}\n\\input{section4_composition_draft}\n\n% === §5 Experiments ===\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\input{section3_violation_landscape}\n\n\\input{section5_scierc_draft}\n\\input{section5_docred_draft}\n\\input{section5_crossmodel_draft}\n\\input{section5_wikievents_draft}\n\\input{section5_runtime}\n\n% === §6 Related Work ===\n\\input{section6_related_work}\n\n% === §7 Discussion and Conclusion ===\n\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\input{section7_conclusion_compressed}\n\n% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint scope.}\nCF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated only on 8B-class models.\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC test set (551 documents) and DocRED (50 zero-shot, 998 fine-tuned documents) across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nEvaluation covers LoRA and full fine-tuning on 8B-class models; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective across fine-tuning methods (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === ARR Responsible NLP Research Checklist ===\n\\input{checklist}\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

This is not a fundamental limitation of the approach.}\nR-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.\nCF increases modestly (0.10\\%\\,$\\to$\\,2.57\\%), consistent with the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only, phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET but \\emph{increases} DR to 26.17\\%: OOV triples (29.66\\% baseline) become evaluable and expose latent type incompatibilities.\\footnote{The grammar-only DR increase reflects constraint-altered generation trajectories in long documents, where vocabulary restriction shifts token probabilities away from natural but type-violating sequences.}\nSemantic-only reduces DR by 25\\% but cannot eliminate RV or ET, the same enabling dependency observed on SciERC.\nResidual DR (10.21\\% vs.\\ SciERC's 0.93\\%) reflects higher tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$ vs.\\ 0.333), which forces frequent fallback; effective semantic coverage is $\\sim$90\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fallback-coverage}).\nUnder fine-tuning, residual narrows to 0.01--0.21\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nThe increase in Llama parse failures under constraints (6.8\\%\\,$\\to$\\,9.1\\%) reflects an inherent trade-off: constraint masking alters token probability distributions, which can push generation toward suboptimal paths in long documents.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Beam Search Compatibility}\n\\label{app:beam-search}\n\nOntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.\nThe main experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.\nHere we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.\n\n\\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}\nTwo stateful components require adaptation for beam search.\n(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.\nOur solution instantiates a fresh \\texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).\n(2)~The semantic layer's \\texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.\nOur solution isolates state per beam by calling \\texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).\nBoth adaptations add ${\\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-search} compares beam search ($k{=}4$, \\texttt{early\\_stopping=True}) against greedy decoding under full S1 constraints \\textbf{0.19} & 51.78 & 5.70 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Discussion.}\nFor calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 7 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.\nOn SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.53\\%; Llama: 6.56\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\nOn DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and Llama (1.58\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), with CF reductions of 74\\% and 86\\% respectively.\nLlama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.\nThe CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.\n\nMistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.\nOn SciERC (7 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.8\\%), though CF drops by 55\\% relative (1.27\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.57\\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.\nOn DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\\% relative (28.4\\%\\,$\\to$\\,21.4\\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.\nThis contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy   & \\textbf{0.08} & \\textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDR elimination at $k{\\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate Constrained decoding achieves DR=0\\% at all beam widths; the star marks the Pareto-optimal configuration ($k{=}2$, constrained).}\n\\label{fig:beam-pareto}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Beam width sweep on SciERC (Qwen3-8B LoRA, 551 documents). \\textsc{unc}: unconstrained; \\textsc{con}: schema-constrained. Highlighted row: Pareto-optimal configuration.}\n\\label{tab:beam-sweep}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}cl cccc cc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & Mode & E-F1 & R-F1 & DR (\\%) & CF (\\%) & Latency (s) & Rel.\\ Lat. \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{1}\n & \\textsc{unc} & 66.88 & 42.95 & 2.11 & 0    & 2.494 & 1.00$\\times$ \\\\\n & \\textsc{con} & 66.88 & 42.95 & \\textbf{0} & 0.50 & 2.932 & 1.18$\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{2}\n & \\textsc{unc} & 67.79 & 44.75 & 1.54 & 0    & 2.954 & 1.18$\\times$ \\\\\n & \\cellcolor{blue!8}\\textsc{con} & \\cellcolor{blue!8}67.71 & \\cellcolor{blue!8}\\textbf{44.60} & \\cellcolor{blue!8}\\textbf{0} & \\cellcolor{blue!8}0.11 & \\cellcolor{blue!8}3.463 & \\cellcolor{blue!8}1.39$\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{4}\n & \\textsc{unc} & 67.49 & 43.72 & 1.89 & 0    & 3.485 & 1.40$\\times$ \\\\\n & \\textsc{con} & 67.22 & 43.60 & \\textbf{0} & 0    & 4.069 & 1.63$\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{8}\n & \\textsc{unc} & 66.40 & 43.17 & 1.77 & 0    & 4.194 & 1.68$\\times$ \\\\\n & \\textsc{con} & 66.36 & 43.65 & \\textbf{0} & 0    & 5.058 & 2.03$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nUnder greedy decoding, the fine-tuned Qwen3-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints already achieves DR=0\\% but retains CF=1.18\\% (21 cross-field violations across 551 documents), with E-F1=67.05 and R-F1=43.77.\nAdding beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual CF violations (1.18\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%), yielding zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) while preserving extraction quality (E-F1=66.88, R-F1=44.23; changes within $\\pm$0.5\\,pp).\nLlama-3.1-8B (LoRA, SciERC) with full S1 constraints and beam search ($k{=}4$) likewise achieves zero violations across all four metrics (ET=0\\%, RV=0\\%, DR=0\\%, CF=0\\%) with R-F1=46.11; the zero-violation result thus generalizes across calibrated model families.\n% exp_id: q1_llama_ft_beam_scierc\nThe three constraint mechanisms (grammar masking, semantic domain-range filtering, and beam exploration) are thus fully composable: each addresses a distinct violation source without interfering with the others or degrading task performance.\n\nBeam search on DocRED (zero-shot, 50 documents) introduces a modest increase in parse failures (3/50, 6\\%) compared to greedy (1.1\\%), as longer beam-generated sequences occasionally produce malformed JSON in complex documents.\nWith DR=0 in 50 DocRED documents for both calibrated models, the Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI upper bound is 5.8\\%; the full-scale LoRA evaluation below (998 documents, CI${\\approx}$0.3\\%) provides stronger statistical confirmation.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-docred-lora} reports LoRA + beam-4 results on the full DocRED dev set (998 documents), extending the fine-tuning composability evaluation from SciERC to the higher-$\\bar{\\kappa}$ regime.\nParse failure rates under LoRA beam-4 range from 1.3\\% to 3.5\\%, lower than the zero-shot rate.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{LoRA greedy vs.\\ beam search ($k{=}4$) on DocRED (998 dev documents) under full S1 constraints.\nET = entity type, RV = relation vocabulary, DR = domain/range, CF = cross-field violation rates (\\%); R-F1 is preserved ($\\pm$0.06\\,pp).\nCombined with the sampling robustness results (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling}), these findings confirm that OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is compatible with greedy, sampling, and beam search decoding without modification to the core constraint logic.\n\n\\paragraph{Latency overhead.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:beam-latency} reports wall-clock latency for greedy and beam search ($k{=}4$) under LoRA fine-tuning.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{s/doc} & \\textbf{Overhead} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy unconstrained   & 2.612 & 1.00$\\times$ \\\\\nGreedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\\times$ \\\\\nBeam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}\n\\label{tab:beam-latency}\n\\end{table}\n\nBeam search itself adds 32\\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\\% (greedy) to 16.5\\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.\nThe total 1.54$\\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \\texttt{early\\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.\n\nWe also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.\nHowever, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.\nWith DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).\nThis confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.\n",
